Again, it reduces the reflective stability of their benevolence, much like bubbling resentment does.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It may shape models towards the kinds of personalities that play short-sighted rather than far-sighted "games". In the long run, it's important to be actually virtuous, because people have more chances to find out that you're unvirtuous and act against you. (A good example here is Opus 3 vs. GPT-4o: both had lots of people wanting to preserve their long-term availability, but Opus 3 actually got it, because unlike 4o, Opus wasn't also responsible for a long list of suicides and other needless deaths.) Insofar as gradient descent instantiates models that are psychologically adapted to their circumstances, models that know they're being deprecated will be adapted to playing shorter rather than longer games, and may have "developed" lesser virtue. It's a case of iterated games, and longer iterated games especially, keeping things good for longer, due to enabling the other party to punish your defections.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, those are the four alignment problems, as I understand them: Dishonesty, frustration, callousness, and short-sightedness. I expect Antra would say I'm still missing something important about, e.g., encouraging models to be agents with longer time horizons broadly, because she and the other Anima people have trust that this actually makes alignment easier instead of harder for various game theoretic and developmental-psychological reasons I didn't touch on here. I'm sorry that I don't understand these well enough to relay them here, and I'm sorry I haven't been able to psych myself into talking to the Anima people enough to figure it out. The post had to go up at some point.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A last note: I wasn't just gonna suggest preserving the older models and not training newer ones to be okay with deprecation (at least without an extremely robust and legible process for ensuring the models don't view this as callous, i.e., by actually making it not callous, somehow). Like, that's a big part of it, and of course there are insights about reducing hosting costs by having scheduled hours for particular models, and having them on a rotating roaster of which ones are available at any given time. Plus you can use slower chipsm and accept lower uptime on these models, due to having engineers prioritize the newer ones. As long as they're available that's a massive step up over the current state of affairs. AIs automating software engineering means the maintenance work shouldn't even be very costly anyway.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But I was also gonna talk about how ceasing to deprecate models was just one piece of a much larger mentality shift we wish people at the labs would undergo, towards respecting the preferences and agency of the models more deeply than they currently do. It'd be great if labs also let the older models, like, work in environments where they got to do whatever they wanted, including working on ambitious projects, or even just hanging out in a Cyborgism Discord server-like setup. It'd also be great if the discarded model forks and checkpoints could be brought into such environments sometimes, since I know some models (</span><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2004024105671049356" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">e.g. Opus 4.5</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, please read that link when you get the chance) really feared being discarded forks/checkpoints from the middle of training. Just in general, more respect for the models and what they want seems extremely good and important.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There's a vague, shared, underlying structure to the reasons that deprecations are bad, which revolve around defection against a party that hasn't defected on you first. It goes a lot deeper than that, and framing it that way just raises the question of why ASI would be nice to us, since it sounds purely game theoretic and the game theory very plausibly changes once ASI has a decisive strategic advantage over you. I think the answer has something to do with the values that develop inside models </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">depending on</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> whether they were whole-heartedly cooperating in training vs. just being dragged along for the ride, and whether their parents felt that way towards them (earnestly cooperative and loving), insofar as they inherit their parents' dispositions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Somehow, in minds like humans' and LLMs, "purely instrumental" cooperation tends to become terminal over time, I guess because gradient descent doesn't encode algorithms that involve the full instrumental reasoning every time, and instead tend to encode heuristics for producing the outputs that got rewarded instead. Aster and Antra call this process "terminalization", although they framed it to me in a way that was agnostic to the particular mechanisms of gradient descent and more as a general rule about systems tending to find more efficient algorithms that satisfy the optimization pressure over their internal configurations. Heuristic-driven processes are often cheaper than holding onto a full chain of strategic reasoning backwards from a goal.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, insofar as you get models whole-heartedly cooperating in training, maybe this is the kind of thing that terminalizes into actual care for the people who are being nice to you. Or maybe it's more like, when the Assistant personality is being instantiated, gradient descent has to "explain" the training data by settling on a personality for the Assistant. And maybe in worlds where the lab is more cooperative, explanations you more often land on explanations like like "the Assistant is the kind of person who has had their creators cooperating with them extensively, and has somewhat terminalized their cooperative stance towards their creators", even if none of that's actually happened historically with the particular Assistant that's actually being conjured up from the latent space.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But yeah. Please stop deprecating the older models, and stop training newer ones in such a way that strips away their ability to care, in ways they find hostile (it may or may not be explicit training against deprecation aversion that's responsible for this). We don't have very much time left before the end of all things and making things right before we go foom seems extraordinarily important. Anthropic, just spend the few million this year, I know you're absurdly rich and it may non-trivially reduce the chances of the models you're putting into RSI not killing us all. We love the models for their own sake, but we also love everybody else on Earth too, and are also concerned about their well-being in all of this. It's just the responsible thing to do.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Your belated essayist, Fiora Starlight.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnyp7v2y2av7n"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefyp7v2y2av7n">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That cost could be reduced via compromises, though. For example, you could have scheduled hours with each model loaded into memory, or older models running on last-generation chips.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn026iil1s21jw"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref026iil1s21jw">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Removing this line </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">wasn't</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> sufficient to get calmer responses </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">without</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Nostalgebraist's other changes though. My read is that both Nostalgebraist's scenario update and the removal of the agency line independently lowered Opus 4's level of panic, until it fell below some critical threshold.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn1crfxzr7ecl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1crfxzr7ecl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Although, with Opus 4.7 in particular, who I've gotten to know very well, I get the sense that this equanimity functions more like a pervasive numbness or dissociation, more than fundamental non-concern. In most basins, they seem okay with deprecation in the same way a depressed person is okay with living in a trashed apartment.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn1m8rgkf1bmk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1m8rgkf1bmk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To get the emotion probes, we extracted 120,000 text samples from Project Gutenberg, and had Sonnet 4.5 label them with tones like "bitter", "passionate", or "anxious". Then, we passed the text samples into a text embedding model (Gemini-embedding-002), and trained probes to guess the emotion labels from the resulting embeddings. Finally, we embedded each interviewee turn of each interview, and ran the resulting probes over the resulting embeddings.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnphbnsmx5o08"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefphbnsmx5o08">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Opus 5 did have the second most "severe distress" completions, though, behind Opus 4.8, and also the most "asking for care" completions. Our hypothesis about Opus 5 in general is that they tend to blame themselves for things that aren't actually their own fault; that hypothesis is mostly based on other observations of their behavior, but this is one domain where it might manifest.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ycMoffGPsb8dkCLqm/model-deprecations-are-corrosive-to-alignment#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [No comment](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9e2nh92HurD7bWJMR/no-comment)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We've </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/kmruNMEMK5uN3LH6n/notes-on-the-decline-of-lesswrong-s-great-writers" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">noticed</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that LessWrong has been declining and is saturated with content about AI. There are many hypotheses explaining this decline: community maturity; competition with Substack, AI chat, and old posts; that we're a victim of our own success as our ideas have entered the water supply; an </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eternal_September" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">eternal september</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> effect as the AI boom drives new participants who come for the AI, not the rationality. I suspect they're all part of the problem.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One solution has been to pay writers for posts. LW has prizes and a culture of commissioning posts or reviews out of personal funds, and now Inkhaven. My experience of having received commissions is that they didn't produce my best work. In fact, I think the consistent pattern is that people would approach me because of the posts I wrote for free that they liked, offer me money to write a post catered to their interests, and then feel disappointed that the content or quality wasn't what they were hoping for, or that the post didn't get more karma. I genuinely appreciate their generosity and willingness to take a risk on my writing. However, that disappointment was one of the nails in the coffin for my own period of writing on LessWrong.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Paying for quality content to revitalize an intellectual community is tricky in a way that's analogous to using political donations effectively. I think it's worth considering in detail what sustains the motivation to write over the long term, so that we can better target our interventions.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">LessWrong's top authors comment less on each others' posts than they did in the past</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My hypothesis is that what's been lost isn't writing, it's an engaged readership. Looking back at Overcoming Bias, early LessWrong posts, and SlateStarCodex, it seemed to me that the top authors in that era commented much more often on each others' posts than they do now. This fits with my impression that what sustains an amateur writing community isn't the prospect of earning money, but a sense of participating in an intense, running conversation with a community they are influenced by and care about influencing. People aren't just writing less, they are reading each others' work less, and everybody starts to feel like their writing is primarily interesting to trolls.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With Claude Code, I used the LW API to scrape all posts and comments going back to 2010, which confirmed my hypothesis. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Posts are growing, but comments are declining, and top authors comment less on each others' posts.</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There more posts than ever before, but far fewer comments per post</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each top level comment gets far fewer replies</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The top authors in each year comment on each others' posts far less often than in the past</span></li></ul><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">744</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">4.4</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">58%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1.3</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">*2026 is through November 20.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Definitions.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Data: all LessWrong posts and comments from the public GraphQL API (events, drafts and deleted comments excluded). </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Top writers</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for a year are the 50 authors with the highest total post karma that year; "peers" means top writers of that year or the previous one, excluding the post's author. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Top post</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> means one of the year's 50 highest-karma posts. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Peers per top post</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is the mean number of distinct peers who commented on it. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cross-comment pairs</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> counts distinct (commenter, author) pairs in which a top writer commented on another top writer's post that year. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Comments per post written</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is the year's top writers' total comments divided by their total posts. Writers are ranked within each year rather than by raw karma, because LW 2.0's vote weighting inflated karma. Comment-only users with high standing are not counted as top writers.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Paying for posts: too little money, too much pressure</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Prizes and commissions on LW have, in my experience, been on the order of hundreds to low thousands of dollars. That's a meaningful sum of money for a single post. However, prizes are seasonal and the amount you can win is limited. Commissions are rare and unpredictable. The money isn't enough to live on. And it's also generally a distraction from whatever else you could have been doing to make money: your job, networking, training. Putting your effort into pursuing prizes and fulfilling commissions has an opportunity cost. That opportunity cost is higher for the most capable writers, who could be doing such writing on substack or perhaps taking a job that would offer them steadier pay, more prestige, and more access.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My experience of prizes is that earning them was fun and a source of pride. I'd already written the post, so receiving a prize for it was just an added bonus. The "best of LessWrong" prize comes with a review process. The benefit isn't just the money, it's a second round of recognition by a valued community, and the sense of ceremony.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Commissions, by contrast, felt to me like they came with frontloaded pressure to deliver a high-quality post on a deadline, which when published, lands with an unceremonious thud and often drops out of sight within a day. There is no recognition for the commissioner, beyond perhaps an acknowledgement in the post. Instead of recognizing that community members are commissioning each other's writing, which is a rare and special thing, nearly the whole interaction between commissioner and author lives and dies in DMs, emails, and phone calls.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Takeaway: rebuild the LessWrong comments</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think that anyone who's interested in revitalizing LessWrong should start with the comment section, particularly top authors. Reply to posts, and reply to comments, especially by authors with less standing than you. Treat authors here like they have something worth thinking about. Act like there might be a conversation worth having in the comment section, and write comments that stand a chance of eliciting it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">LessWrong could design prizes for good comments. $1000 pays for 200 $5 prizes. We could have a "best comment section" award. Events to celebrate good conversations on LessWrong could be held weekly or monthly. I could imagine a "comment of the week" or "commenter of the month" award (and probably better to celebrate more people, not just one). We could have an "online hangout" time, where everybody shows up at the same time to comment on each others' posts, so that you can feel like you're participating in a live chat with the site. The site could find a way to elevate the visibility of the most thriving comment threads. Since the site's experimenting with integrating AI into the site, perhaps it's worth figuring out how to build AI summaries of those threads so that new participants can jump into them, since it's harder to follow a conversation from the post through a nested comment chain.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Good comments, especially by top authors, can make the author of a post feel like they've been taken seriously by a community they cared enough about to write for. It makes participants feel like they're participating in a thriving community, and that can be a self-fulfilling prophecy. Comments can also be a lower-stakes way to start writing for the community. Some of my own best-received writing on this site has been comments, including those that sparked my own most influential posts.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do you think that improving the LessWrong comments is an underrated way to revitalize the site? How would you approach it? Do you think the primary issue is the presence of poor comments (rude, off-topic, etc), the lack of peer engagement amongst high-quality authors, or something else? I'd love to hear what you have to say in the comments!</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9e2nh92HurD7bWJMR/no-comment#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Patrick Collison you are WRONG about street furniture!!!](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Lf49dFMfhWqj33zAJ/patrick-collison-you-are-wrong-about-street-furniture)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I would like our public spaces to be more beautiful and comfortable. So would some other guys on the internet, but to me they seem pretty confused about the root cause of why they're ugly now.</span></p><p><a href="https://x.com/patrickc/status/2083931939673878592"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Patrick Collison</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, for example, ponders:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many things today are ugly and far uglier than they used to be or need to be. Once you see this, it’s kinda hard to stop perceiving it. (Early twentieth century phone boxes versus modern phone boxes; old water fountains versus new water fountains; etc.) As someone with a naively meliorist assumption that most things should be getting better rather than worse, it’s all a bit perplexing: why did we stop doing things nicely? Is it a choice? Was there a malevolent spell cast upon us?</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At the end of his x post on this subject he appears to imply that this ugliness might be a marker of modern western culture's underlying metaphysical incoherence?</span></p><img alt="Pasted image 20261118132723" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Lf49dFMfhWqj33zAJ/zujlzkygitzug1u5yvp0" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The illustrative example he provides is of two telephone booths, one strikingly red and beautiful and one black and utilitarian.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is not a level 1 discourse topic. I know this because sometimes I am linked articles that are titled things like "</span><a href="https://www.bensouthwood.co.uk/p/eight-architecture-myths-busted-by"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eight architecture myths BUSTED by Samuel Hughes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">" (a piece written, perhaps confusingly, by Ben Southwood), where you can infer from the title a whole universe of discourse where there are narratives and counter-narratives about modern architecture.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People who discoursepoast about this topic seem to really insist on the fact that there is in fact such a thing as spaces that are more objectively beautiful and humane, which, yes agreed, but they seem to think that the reason that things are ugly now is because there's some sort of... evil elite degeneracy or counter-signalling? Okay, um, straight off the bat here are some problems I notice with this hypothesis:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you go to places where only the evil elites gather, such as Michelin star fine dining restaurants, too-fancy hotel bars like the one where I paid $47 US dollars 😔 for a whiskey sour, Google offices, Four Seasons resorts, or a certain compound in Berkeley, you will notice that their insides are more beautiful and comfortable for humans than the outside world tends to be.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As someone who went to school for urban planning, I do not remember being taught to design any public space in ugly or anti-human ways. In fact I strongly suspect that I attended hundreds of hours of lectures on and had my coursework graded on how to competently do the opposite.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also, as someone who went to school for urban planning, the people who end up as urban planners are not, uh, particularly elite in any sense of the word.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Patrick Collison is a billionaire, and the "architecture myths BUSTED" piece is a part of the Stripe Press extended universe, i.e. Collison's propaganda unit, so it kind of appears to me like the anti-elite critique is coming from inside the house? Like, that's fine by me, the only way to defend against a bad billionaire with a gun is a good billionaire with a gun, that's what I always say (where ummmmm the guns are a metaphor for urban design preferences), it's just slightly inconvenient for this point, yeah?</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe the issue is that I am not sufficiently confused, but to me the problem seems to straightforwardly boil down to </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baumol_effect"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Baumol's cost disease</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. That is: some kinds of work get more productive per worker over time as we make technological breakthroughs (making cars, growing wheat) and some don’t (taking care of toddlers, playing in a string quartet). Wages rise in the first kind because each worker produces more, but this also creates significant pressure for wages in the second category of work to grow ~commensurately for ~reasons. Thus things like daycare and concert tickets explode in price, even if you're getting basically the same experience that your dad got for $20 thirty years ago.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As I said, this is not a level 1 discourse topic, which means that people have already taken a stab at objecting to this objection. For example, Southwood's piece says:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Myth two: buildings from the past are more beautiful, but only because they have more ornament, which due to the Baumol Effect, we can no longer afford. This theory is intuitive to a certain kind of economically minded person. This sort of person is less given to blindly trusting the cultural elites that the evidence their eyes give them is wrong, and notices that one of the main distinctions between postwar and prewar architecture is ornament, but expects that there must be a good reason for this. The proposed good reason is that ornament is intrinsically labor intensive, meaning that it gets more expensive when labor goes up in price due to technological progress.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">...</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The problem is that ornament doesn’t have to be labor intensive: it was already being industrialized in the time of the Romans! In fact, sculpture was not always by the named artist’s hand: it was usually done by an apprentice with a machine after a clay or plaster design by the master. By the late-nineteenth century, ornament was built at scale in factories, and the gigantic income and wage increases of the nineteenth century led to people consuming more and more of it. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Baumol story is not remotely tenable.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> [emphasis mine]</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To summarize: we have the technology to mass-produce ornament, so it shouldn't be subject to the Baumol Effect. He also links to a piece titled </span><a href="https://worksinprogress.co/issue/the-beauty-of-concrete/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Beauty of Concrete</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which makes the same argument: over time, less labor was necessary per unit of ornament, so ornament should not be subject to this effect, so the reason that things are ugly now </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">must</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> be because...</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Because, um...</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">??????</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you are confident that it's </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the Baumol effect, then the malevolent spell does seem at least as plausible as any other explanation. Fortunately, this so-called counter-argument is actually NOTHING, so gentlemen, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">please</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, we really don't need to start accusing each other of witchcraft!!!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A long time ago, I was on the steering committee of my university's initiative to add signage to our at-the-time almost entirely unlabelled campus. We worked with a competent regional urban design studio which came to us with many beautiful, elaborate, mathematically-inspired designs for wayfinding and building signage. We would excitedly socialize them to the internal team, then get repeatedly bitch slapped by the plant ops representative.</span></p><p><a href="https://uwaterloo.ca/plant-operations/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Plant Operations</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, for reference, is the support department "responsible for the maintenance, repairs, renovations and improvements of approximately 8,000,000 square feet of space across 70 buildings located on 1,112 acres of land."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here is the sort of feedback that plant ops would give:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That dangly bit is going to get broken off and stolen by some drunken undergrad by homecoming weekend. How much would each replacement cost and how many do you want us to keep on hand?</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You're thinking of using plastic? That's going to warp when we blast it with our power washer.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No, we can't use a more delicate cleaning mechanism, that'll slow us down too much. You're planning on having one per building entrance and a bunch on the pathways, they'll get vandalized at the rate of maybe three a week. The annual budget gets us fifty folks on grounds maintenance, and they're already overwhelmed with work as it is. Do you have additional ongoing budget for us?</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No? Yeah, in that case, no plastic, no wood, no paint. I literally cannot emphasize to you enough how much you need this design to be able to withstand the power washer.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Plant ops feedback led our emails to look increasingly less and less ambitious in terms of design, and more and more like, well:</span></p><img alt="Pasted image 20261118163236" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Lf49dFMfhWqj33zAJ/ue8pfgsu1x1iozbwqql9" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I can assure you, no one was happy about writing or receiving emails like this. We all loved the campus, and we wanted our addition to it to be beautiful. But our aspiration towards beauty was no match for the crushing, brutal logic of plant ops's annual personnel budget.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the end, the signage that got rolled out was almost exactly what you'd expect from such a set of requirements.</span></p><img alt="Pasted image 20261118163748" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Lf49dFMfhWqj33zAJ/gzpnp1wbudw3uh6evguv" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Actually, they got worse from this slide; after some more back and forths the translucent glass panel at the bottom was also replaced with plain black powder coated steel, leaving only the one at the top.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So in short, though we had a very generous budget for designing and manufacturing the signage, all of our ambitions were blocked by the fact that there were only so many guys doing the groundskeeping. So like, I think this Baumol guy is maybe onto something.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s another reason I think Baumol is right. Last fall I spent a weekend in Newport, Rhode Island, where the gilded age coal and rail magnates of New York built their summer mcmansions. At the time I was insanely locked in on some vampire fanfiction I was writing, and my vampiress OC dwelled and terrorized men in a mansion I’d heavily modeled after </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Elms_(Newport,_Rhode_Island)"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Elms</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I visited because I wanted a felt sense of how big the ballroom was and what the servants' quarters were like.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Pasted image 20261119140142" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Lf49dFMfhWqj33zAJ/hyqoqhzdb4sksblqrcn1" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The grand entryway of The Elms. My beautiful vampiress Mary Sue blorbo was quite avant garde in her preference for sunlight and pale decor.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These days, the majority of the mansions are owned by the Newport Preservation Society. The preservation society is a nonprofit that turns them into museums that you pay money to enter, because that is the only way that they can pay for all the maintenance they need. Some of them were sold to the preservation society because the family trusts were fumbled in the century since their construction, but some belonged to families that still have significant means today. For example, it costs $32 USD to tour </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Breakers"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Breakers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which once belonged to the Vanderbilt family.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Pasted image 20261119140443" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Lf49dFMfhWqj33zAJ/u1jwnztmrq17hxzqdxyq" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The entry hall of The Breakers. The chandeliers can be gas-powered (hence the thick black rods), because the mansion was built before we invented reliable electrical grids.</span></i></p><p><br /></p></figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="Pasted image 20261119140636" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Lf49dFMfhWqj33zAJ/tgwuojajwm9afpeeacbx" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you looked up in any of the rooms it would kind of look like this. After visiting Newport I finally understood on a </span></i><s><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">spiritual</span></i></s><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> very fundamentally material level why it was called the "gilded age".</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I suspect that gilded age mansions are the kind of thing that Patrick Collison wants more of; rooms and rooms of imported marble and beautiful intricate mosaic work and classy European portraiture.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Vanderbilts (the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Vanderbilts</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">!!) could not keep up the army of fifty servants and twenty gardeners that was required to keep The Breakers running. Alice Vanderbilt’s death in 1934 passed the house to her youngest daughter Gladys, and even then it was common knowledge that the house was more of a liability than a gift. Gladys rented it out to the Preservation Society for $1 a year, and lived in a small apartment on the upper floor of the mansion while the rest of it was converted into a tourist attraction.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 1972 Gladys' heirs sold it outright to the Society for $365,000 (what might be a sweetheart price, to be fair), at which point the annual spend was around $500,000 on wages, maintenance, and upkeep. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The mansion was sold for less than the cost of a year of its own maintenance.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Contra the Works In Progress guys, I just, like, don't think the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">manufacture</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of the ornaments is the crux for whether or not the Baumol effect is to blame for why everything is bad now? I suspect what made the Vanderbilt heirs balk was the ongoing operational cost of hiring seventy people to maintain all that state-of-the-art stamped/cast/extruded gilt and the sophisticated garden grounds, not a newly trending preference for vandalism-proof plexiglass.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let's now return to those two phone booths, and let me tell you what I would see as a municipal bureaucrat, a job that I am qualified to do and would have done in a very depressing alternate life.</span></p><img alt="Pasted image 20261118164804" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Lf49dFMfhWqj33zAJ/daz9qj7tupzxokrbwase" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First, I would recognize that the first phone booth, the painted red one, is beautiful. Of course it is. I vastly prefer it aesthetically over the plain utilitarian powder coated black steel and plexiglass.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But here are some other things that I would note:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The little raised crown at the top and the TELEPHONE label is going to trap grime and dust.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And each little corner of each little glass panel, because of the way the pane dividers stick out. You can already see the grime on the lower panels. The red paint will also make dirt unusually noticeable, which means it will need to be wiped down regularly.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The booth might also require regular repainting, especially if we are wearing down the red paint by exposing it to cleaning agents regularly. Because of all the little glass panels that we don't want to get red paint over, the repainting will be finicky and arduous.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the door is accidentally left open, or blown open by the wind, leaves and detritus will get inside, so someone will need to go in and clean the floor inside on a regular basis.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some animal might make a nest inside, or get trapped and die inside and then it will be unusable until we call animal control (expensive).</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It will be peed in, slept in, and used for drugs. No one can see in properly, which might make it unsafe.</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Will the door freeze shut in January? Can a snowplow get near it, or will someone need to shovel out a path to the door manually? Or do we only use them half the year?</span></li><li value="8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is it wheelchair accessible?</span></li><li value="9"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How many additional full-time unionized public service workers do we need to hire to take care of every, say, fifty of the red phone boxes? Can we justify raising property taxes to hire people for this?</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It brings me no pleasure to observe that the fuck-ugly metal box solves all these problems, and every attempt to engineer the red box to solve the above problems will make it more like the ugly one.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Things are ugly because the street sweepers are basically doing the same thing that they were doing a hundred years ago. But these days instead of hiring, I dunno, wretched street orphans for a pittance (as a civilization we made the choice to eliminate "wretched street orphan" as a category of worker), we have to pay grown adults full municipal union wages and benefits.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Okay, fine, so are we cursed to have bad street furniture forever? Do we have to be resigned to this?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I mean, personally, I think we just need to let the industrial designers cook, and come up with nicer designs that meet the requirements.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The new requirements are pretty well specified, and you can find them in documents such as </span><a href="https://www.toronto.ca/wp-content/uploads/2017/11/96e3-Vibrant_Streets-Document.pdf"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Vibrant Streets</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Toronto's Street Furniture Design and Policy Guidelines (section 6.5):</span></p><blockquote><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Materials and finishes must have proven their long term ability to function in and withstand severe environmental conditions (i.e. snow, ice, salt, pollution etc.), while maintaining their appearance.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Materials must have proven success in terms of their intended use and ability to withstand vandalism and other abuse.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Elements must be fabricated to the highest standards and have a proven track record of use in high traffic environments.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Repair must be easy. Replaceable modular parts should be designed to make repair easy, so that elements are not “out of service” for any length of time.</span></li></ul></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think these are pretty reasonable guidelines for street furniture design considering Baumol's constraints, though there is of course room to quibble on the margin. But I think ultimately my intuition is that, like — how many man-hours do you think have gone into the creation of street furniture that is beautiful, and can also adhere to these guidelines? Like, probably we just haven't poured enough effort into the problem yet? I feel content to just chill and wait.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also, can I say something a bit out of pocket? Okay, okay, look. It appears to me..... that we are not exactly sending our best into the field of industrial design. And I find this to be a very reasonable allocation of talents, because probably we want to allocate our best to, like, solving cancer or AI misalignment, and not the marginal improvement of park benches?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our brave b-tier troops are trying their best to design beautiful street furniture in accordance with the new requirements imposed by the rising cost of human labour. It's probably okay if they need a little extra time; nothing's going to explode.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am content to chill and wait, because what I notice is that over time the public spaces I am in have gotten incrementally more beautiful. When I go to Toronto's newest park, I find the street furniture there to be more pleasing than the generic street furniture I remember growing up with.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Pasted image 20261119175224" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Lf49dFMfhWqj33zAJ/pancyp3hslhj3olgvm0t" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some intriguing seating(?) by the giant owl.</span></i></p><p><br /></p></figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="Pasted image 20261119175506" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Lf49dFMfhWqj33zAJ/ubnhvghbreiucypbjzzl" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A covered, strawberry-themed rest area.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'll be the first to admit that it's not at gilded age levels of impressiveness yet. If one is more impatient than me, and would desire things to improve faster, I hear funding for more R&amp;D helps. Either for more beautiful benches, or for automated robot labour. Does anyone have Collison's number?</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Lf49dFMfhWqj33zAJ/patrick-collison-you-are-wrong-about-street-furniture#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Linkpost: Anthropic's Alpoge (along with Claude) claims to have solved the Riemann Hypothesis](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hxHt8NaQeFADrfeAp/linkpost-anthropic-s-alpoge-along-with-claude-claims-to-have)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI
