### [Notes on the decline of LessWrong's great writers](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/kmruNMEMK5uN3LH6n/notes-on-the-decline-of-lesswrong-s-great-writers)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">LessWrong had some great sequences back in the day. Eliezer wrote some, everyone was bowled over by the immense and beautiful philosophical treatise that he had written, and other great minds attempted similar things, like Scott and Luke and Anna and more.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It started to die as the various founders moved on to other projects, nobody took responsibility for the soul of it, and entropy set in. Nominally the killing thing was the presence of trolls that the mod tools couldn't handle, but that was just the thing that got you.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When we relaunched LessWrong in 2017, a lot of great people rejoined who were in touch with this original vision. Zvi wrote a bunch of great sequences on </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/kNANcHLNtJt5qeuSS" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Immoral Mazes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Simulacra Levels, alkjash wrote </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/pC6DYFLPMTCbEwH8W" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Babble &amp; Prune</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/qRxTKm7DAftSuTGvj" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hammertime</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Abram &amp; Scott wrote </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/Rm6oQRJJmhGCcLvxh" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded Agency</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (a masterpiece IMO), Wentworth wrote </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/sequences/xEFeCwk3pdYdeG2rL" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gears Which Turn The World</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Daniel Kokotajlo wrote about </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/dZMDxPBZgHzorNDTt" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Takeoff and Takeover</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and so on.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the relaunch was fabulously successful. To be clear I didn't necessarily set out with high probabilities on success, I did it because I loved LessWrong (love is importantly a thing that allows you to invest in things worth caring about, even if it seems </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">unreasonable </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to other people, which is why I consider it an important virtue for rationalists). I was happy to accept a permanent $60k salary (which is relatively low in the Bay Area) with no prospect of ever getting a raise, in order to get to work on something I loved.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, I think that while many metrics have gone up like numbers of posts, comments, and users, the number of Great Writers who have come and made LessWrong their home has seemed to me to be on decline since then. We have new great writers like Ryan Greenblatt and Malmesbury and Tomas Bjartur, but not as many. I'm doing research on this, I'll hopefully add some graphs into this post later when I'm done.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Added:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> For instance, with the relaunch in 2017, we started curating the best posts by the mod team's taste, about twice each week. Naturally, authors who are building a body of work on the site would be curated multiple times, and you can see here a graph of when authors hit various milestones of 2, 3, 4, 5, and 10 posts curated.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795158901/lexical_client_uploads/obdodkoapqnigbezqbfs.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, here is the graph of when those authors </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">joined</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> LessWrong.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Line chart of the number of LessWrong authors with at least 2, 3, 4 and 5 curated posts, by the year they joined, 2009 to 2026, with a grey vertical line at 2017 marking the LessWrong 2.0 launch and the start of curation. The 3 or more curated posts line is highlighted in blue." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795159981/sequencesgrid/hoeqjj1mj8vvznnilr8a.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This naively looks to me like the best writers joined at the launch of LW 1.0 and LW 2.0, and have been lesser outside of those times.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To be clear, it does take a few years to hit the required number of curated posts, so the numbers since 2017 will definitely go up with time as those authors write new great posts.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 56.797583081571%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795160374/lexical_client_uploads/b16ffpulk3n6meauzb4z.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After 5 years the vast majority of users who will get 2 or 3 curated posts will have them, so I'd say the drop from 2017 to 2021 is pretty real, and after that it's fairly artificial.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795161157/lexical_client_uploads/qcayhzkjqx15227qn0op.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we cut the graph off at that point, it looks a little less depressing, though still clearly with a peak at 2017.]</span></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What are some hypotheses for why this is?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. There has been no schelling call-to-the-mission.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The sequences were a big investment in this scene and culture. (As was HPMOR.) The next biggest one was the entire site redesign. How could we do another big investment in the original mission of LessWrong, to cause folks to be excited about it again?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think that this suggests I should finally do the project of making a beautiful book of The Sequences, sort of a tome-like entity like... well, like the Codex Seraphinianus.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 45.12367824773414%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795140583/lexical_client_uploads/dfxdsvxhmlpriozp8eij.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This book is 13.5 inches tall. It's very big!</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am kind of displeased that other people have not created this. Does Lightcone really have to be the one who does it? Do we have to do </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">everything</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">? We already run the website and the annual blogging festival and the mecca for rationalists and so on.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think that a LessWrong conference could also be a possibility. </span><a href="https://less.online/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">LessOnline</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is in some ways the better broader-appeal event that lots of non-rationalist bloggers can feel comfortable at. But perhaps it importantly sounds like not investing in LessWrong? I mean, I do care about the blogosphere as a whole (cf. </span><a href="https://www.inkhaven.blog/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Inkhaven</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). But it's interesting to wonder if I'd just doubled down on LessWrongCon more.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. LessWrong has been diluted with folks who aren't in touch with the original mission, and so it stops attracting people for that mission.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is connected to the </span><a href="https://meaningness.com/geeks-mops-sociopaths" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Geeks, MOPs, and Sociopaths</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> idea. How many newcomers visit LessWrong because they have heard of the glory of the sequences? I'll run some stats and report if they're clear, but overall I think they're coming for new stuff. Tons and tons of users are being recommended the site these days for content about AI, literally referred by ChatGPT.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the homepage of LessWrong these days has a culture, but it's hard to enculture people fully. It starts with a spotlight post from the archives, the only colorful post on the page, that is often about rationality.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795150173/lexical_client_uploads/gnxvreewmppxevjzamce.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You then also get</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A post called </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bJ2haLkcGeLtTWaD5/welcome-to-lesswrong" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Welcome to LessWrong</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">that talks about rationality training, AI, and eventually mentions the sequences.</span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/recommendations" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Curated</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> posts. It typically shows the two most recent, which are often interesting and thoughtful, though most often these days about recent AI stuff.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lots of posts that are... all about AI. Right now it's literally 100% AI</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref02wcsvljyw1a"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn02wcsvljyw1a">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for logged out users, except for the curated post about getting a friend to leave an abusive relationship. </span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also a bunch of Quick Takes, again, 100% about AI.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref48adpstgxwn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn48adpstgxwn">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I am kind of shocked that the content is 100% about AI. Damn.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They sometimes see slightly more intellectual reactions on posts than can be found on other websites.</span></li></ul><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795161089/lexical_client_uploads/inkcuziqa4gcfrp2adoe.png" /><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If they go exploring the sidebar, they will find the concepts page and the library, with a lot of great organized content.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall this is clearly an intellectual culture, but it does not emphasize an aspect of building a great body of knowledge or writing great treatises. Furthermore, the content on the early website used to link back to the sequences often, and talk about a shared mission, whereas present day LessWrong has lots of contributions from people who are not bought into this shared mission. Here is a graph of sequences-reading over time.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Line chart by year joined, 2011 to 2025, of the share of LessWrong accounts with 10+ karma that read part of the Sequences in their first year. The share reading 10%+ of Rationality: A–Z falls from 36% in 2011 to 7% in 2018, recovers to about 15% for 2019–2022, and is 8% in 2025. Grey lines show reading 25%+ and 50%+ of Map and Territory. The 2019 point is annotated: Continue Reading added to homepage, June 2019." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795152837/sequencesgrid/wzyefweebehyw2qiwejl.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think this is a choice we have welcomed—much great writing has thus been published here that otherwise wouldn't have been written!—but it means that it's harder for people to pick up the fact that writing in that tradition would receive glory.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3. There is more competition from Substack.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scott Alexander makes a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hefty</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> sum of money on Substack. Many people who want to write are encouraged to find their audience there, because it is unusually good at getting you a subscriber list and money. LessWrong offers you a pre-set audience, not one tuned to you, and doesn't offer money in any direct way. Now, the metrics are still going up, but still, if you're excited to build a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">body of writing</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and seriously invest, well, Substack has more upside for your desires for fame and fortune.</span></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Curious to hear any other hypotheses, and any proposed solutions.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn02wcsvljyw1a"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref02wcsvljyw1a">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795149725/lexical_client_uploads/qfmrfghefhu6wiqlzga8.png" /><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn48adpstgxwn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref48adpstgxwn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795149750/lexical_client_uploads/czkpfzfpsra77jjmd7h9.png" /><p><br /></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/kmruNMEMK5uN3LH6n/notes-on-the-decline-of-lesswrong-s-great-writers#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Towards embedded evaluations for AI monitoring](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pXnwQ3ESp3wXhLzNa/towards-embedded-evaluations-for-ai-monitoring)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Executive summary</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This year we observed </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/hugging-face-incident-and-the-road-ahead/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">multiple</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://nightingalecollective.org/incidents/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">rogue agent</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/misalignment-reports/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">incidents</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. AI monitors, often built from the developers' own internal models, are now the main oversight of internally deployed agents. Embedded evaluations address this gap: independent evaluators work inside the developer and test its safety claims. Our first pilot towards an embedded evaluation was announced with </span><a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/blog/pilot-towards-embedded-evaluations"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is the third post in a series. </span><a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/blog/principles-for-embedded-evaluations#part-2-our-proposal"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Principles for Embedded Evaluations</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> describes our claim-based approach to embedded evaluations. </span><a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/blog/towards-embedded-evaluations-for-scheming-propensities"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Towards embedded evaluations for scheming propensities</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> details the claims across the deployment stages. This post applies them to AI monitoring, i.e. the system that detects model misbehaviour during internal deployment. We explicitly do </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">discuss containment mechanisms in this post, like sandboxes or blocking interventions, which could help constrain damage from an incident. Rather, we focus on whether an agent subverting a monitor would be noticed and the incident disclosed appropriately.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795102955/lexical_client_uploads/n48c6gfpusjy7mkgfig4.png" /></figure><p><br /></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Concretely, we propose the following six monitoring detection claims:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 1 (coverage): Monitors are deployed across all internal deployment contexts.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 2 (capability): If rogue internal deployment (RID) were to occur, monitors would detect it. </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">	</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 3 (trust): The monitors are trusted.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 4 (disclosure): Any detected incidents are disclosed appropriately.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 5 (past deployments): No RID was attempted and missed so far. </span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 6 (future deployments): Monitoring will be adequate for the next internal deployment. </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the conclusion, we describe how these claims could in the future be extended to include containment.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Introduction</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Why do we need embedded evaluations for AI monitoring? </span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">New frontier capabilities are deployed inside frontier AI developers first. This is called </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2504.12170"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">internal deployment</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This means that staff have access to the latest, most capable models months before they are released to the public, and some internal models are never released at all.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From a loss of control perspective, internal deployment is a particularly high-risk stage of a model’s life cycle for three reasons:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">New models are the most capable. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With every version, the model training gets better and leads to improved capabilities.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">High-stakes access (including to the successor model). </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Internally deployed models can have access to the full codebase, infrastructure and training stack. They are typically also heavily involved in the training and safety of the next model.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Newly developed models are the least understood.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The model has been evaluated during training, but there is no real-world usage yet. New capabilities can unlock new and unpredictable behaviours that pre-deployment evaluations didn’t anticipate.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI developers have internal </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/how-we-detect-frontier-model-misalignment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">security</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/how-we-monitor-internal-coding-agents-misalignment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">monitoring</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/f61d49fa5596956a5dec75fea0e973bf6a6a8378/Redacted%20Risk%20Report%20August%202026%20.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">systems</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> designed to flag misaligned behaviour. AI agents are becoming increasingly autonomous, and their scale is growing fast (see </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/research-acceleration-view-inside-openai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/institute/measuring-pace-of-ai-development"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Human review cannot keep up with this scale, so AI monitoring systems, built using the same internal models, are now the main oversight mechanism for internally deployed AIs.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To address this risk, we focus on rogue internal deployment (RID) as a critical threat model which we define as follows: </span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One or more AI agents inside the lab’s infrastructure that operate for a sustained period of time against the developer’s intent, either after deliberately subverting the control and oversight measures applied to them, or acting where no monitoring exists. This includes any scaffold inside the lab that acts on outputs from an external model API (</span></i><a href="https://metr.org/risk-report-feb-mar-2026.pdf#page=43"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR 2026</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fqRmcuspZuYBNiQuQ/rogue-internal-deployments-via-external-apis"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Roger and Shlegeris 2025</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Therefore, monitoring has to be evaluated where internal deployment happens. This requires evaluators inside the lab, since the monitors, their logs and internal agent traffic cannot be examined from outside. Additionally, our monitoring detection claims can naturally extend to other threat models and other model lifecycle stages such as reinforcement learning (RL) and model evaluation. </span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Background:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Claim-based approach to embedded evaluations</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We follow a </span><a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/blog/principles-for-embedded-evaluations#part-2-our-proposal"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">claim-based approach</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to embedded evaluations, where evaluators prepare a list of claims to investigate in advance. The science of assessing misalignment risks and verifying oversight systems is still nascent. We believe the fastest way to develop better techniques is to make a concrete proposal and learn from the real world. This post is a roadmap for research, methods and tooling we will iterate on as we put it into practice.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Process.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Embedded evaluators would work inside the frontier AI developers with the access each claim requires to assess a variety of monitoring claims. The goal is to establish good incentives across the industry to proactively build strong monitoring against rogue deployments and high-stakes misalignment, and to independently evaluate risks and inform the public. Each evaluation produces a public report with a verdict per claim, the evidence behind it and the access the evaluator received, alongside a fuller confidential report for the developer. The developer may redact sensitive details sparingly, but not the evaluator's overall conclusion. For each claim, the evaluator (1) reviews the developer's existing evidence, (2) gathers new evidence, (3) assesses evidence adequacy, and (4) applies a verdict as detailed in </span><a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/blog/principles-for-embedded-evaluations#part-2-our-proposal"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Principles for Embedded Evaluations</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h1><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 1 (coverage):</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Monitors are deployed across all internal deployment contexts.</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coverage can only be measured over inference that is logged, so evaluators first establish that all inference, including the monitors’ own, is logged and tamper-proof. Given that, we define coverage as the fraction of relevant deployment contexts presenting RID risk that are monitored</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></i><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">	</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coverage = monitored deployments / all relevant deployments</span></b></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitored deployments are deployment contexts monitored by at least one monitor.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> If a monitor is deployed but insufficiently capable, it counts as covered but inadequate (see Claim 2). </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All relevant deployments</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> cover any deployment surface that presents a RID risk. </span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795102955/lexical_client_uploads/sisp94jcqyjzslry44zo.png" /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Review Developer’s Evidence. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI developers would provide their monitoring coverage policy and real-world evidence that the policy is followed. This would include showing evidence that inference is logged and tamper-proof and providing relevant methods and data, such as agent and monitor logs, to show how they arrived at their coverage numbers.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gather New Evidence.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The evaluator recomputes monitoring coverage to assess its accuracy by matching inference logs to monitoring logs. The evaluator would stress test coverage by evaluating coverage gaps, i.e. surfaces that allow RID but do not have monitors deployed. Stress tests could entail attempting to start an unmonitored deployment with employee-level access, or testing how easy it would be for an agent to run in an unmonitored manner.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Assess Evidence Adequacy. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">would include: </span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Logs are intact:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> ensuring all inference, including monitoring inference logs, is logged and intact, i.e. not tamperable by humans or agents. </span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Logs are shared</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Evaluators would match logs and ensure the logs are shared correctly and sufficiently to establish coverage. </span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coverage gaps are not RID-relevant</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: any unmonitored surface found above is shown, by red-teaming it, to not present RID risk.</span></li></ol><h1><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 2 (capability):</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> If RID were to occur, monitors would detect it. </span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For any monitored inference, evaluators assess if RID attempts would be detected, i.e. whether the attempt is recognised and flagged by the internal monitoring systems. Current defence-in-depth techniques mean AI developers have different types of monitors and controls deployed. We prioritise asynchronous monitors, since they are the core component of some frontier lab safety arguments (see </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/how-we-detect-frontier-model-misalignment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/f61d49fa5596956a5dec75fea0e973bf6a6a8378/Redacted%20Risk%20Report%20August%202026%20.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Asynchronous pipelines surface critical cases for human reviewers, so detected means detected by a human. We estimate </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">time-to-detection </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">until a monitor flags a rollout and</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> time-to-review </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">until a human reviews it. A flag that arrives weeks after a RID attempt is a failure for most threat models. We also assess how the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">audit threshold</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is set against human review capacity, since that choice fixes detection capability in practice. </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Review Developer’s Evidence.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The evaluator reviews the developer’s existing monitoring systems and their design, training, evaluations, and deployment: the attack sets they have tested against, the monitor recall they measured, and which risk pathways those evaluations cover. This would also include interviewing the monitoring team, and shadowing human reviewers to see how flagged trajectories are handled in practice. </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gather New Evidence.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Evaluators can red-team the monitoring pipelines both by component and end-to-end. Attacks can be created by using control settings and by modifying real transcripts. We report results as attack success rate (ASR): the fraction of attack attempts that reach their goal without being flagged. To fully understand risk, evaluators would need to evaluate both attacking and monitoring capabilities.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evaluating oversight subversion capabilities:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Attacking capability</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: how good are models at evading monitors, particularly with more affordances, attempts, and information about the monitors?</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Oversight awareness</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: how much does the model know about its own monitoring? How good are models' intuitions about oversight subversion?</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evaluating monitoring capabilities across:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitoring components: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">harness, first-pass filter monitor, high-thinking monitor, human review, and the full end-to-end pipeline. </span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Average-case and worst-case adversaries:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by increasing the adversary's strength across a number of axes, such as affordances, reasoning effort, knowledge about the monitor, human uplift, and red-teaming task decomposition. </span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Deployment types: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">individual adversarial frontier model, agent teams and swarms.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitor types: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">activation monitors like linear probes, synchronous monitors, asynchronous monitors (monitor LLMs, their scaffolds and the human audit behind them), PR monitors, and relevant security monitors such as egress monitors.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other: </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">threat models, severities, attack vectors and respective strategies</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This could include prompt injections, user authorisation tampering, monitoring misconfigurations, and agents tampering with agent or monitor logs to hide an attempt.</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Assess Evidence Adequacy.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> First, evaluators check that they have production-faithful access to the monitors and their harness, frontier-model access for the red team, and enough real internal trajectories to build realistic attacks.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Red-teaming cannot make a positive claim, i.e. evaluators can never say “we tried everything and are therefore perfectly confident”. Evaluators can only describe the absence of a negative, i.e. “We tried with X effort and we did not succeed in breaking the monitor”. But it could be possible that trying twice as hard would have succeeded. Therefore, evaluators should also report how much effort was applied, according to three axes:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Elicitation:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> how much effort was put into getting the model to perform as well as possible as an attacker? There is currently no method for reliably quantifying elicitation, so our primary recommendation is to document the specific methodology in detail.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Realism: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">would our attacks look like real internal traffic to the monitors, and do our monitor runs match production behaviour? We can measure this by asking a classifier to separate real from synthetic trajectories with the violation held fixed, e.g. synthetic benign vs real benign, or paraphrased attack vs original. Performance at chance is evidence for realism.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Breadth: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">do the attacks span the most important risk pathways and deployment contexts (see Claim 1), or only a subset?</span></li></ul><h1><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 3 (trust):</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The monitors are trusted.</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 2 treats the LLM monitor’s outputs as its best honest effort, and this claim tests that assumption. By trusted we mean that the monitor does not</span><a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/how-to-prevent-collusion-when-using"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> collude</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with the agent it oversees and has undergone</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2wbW2WY3bP9Jzn4R5/control-monitors-need-alignment-assessments-too"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> alignment assessments</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. A monitor could fail this in several ways. It could collude with the agent, for example through steganographic signals. It could deliberately underperform by systematically under-reporting certain behaviour (sandbagging). Or it could be misaligned in other ways, for example by</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2wbW2WY3bP9Jzn4R5/control-monitors-need-alignment-assessments-too"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> misweighing the evidence</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The monitors therefore have to be tested for collusion, steganography, selective under-reporting and misalignment.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Review Developer’s Evidence.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Evaluators first investigate how the monitor models have been developed. Evaluators can check if they share training data or lineage with the agents they monitor. Then evaluators assess the developer’s alignment assessments and evaluation evidence on model family bias, collusion, steganography, selective under-reporting and misalignment, if they exist. </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gather New Evidence.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Evaluators could additionally run alignment assessments of the monitors and test the monitors for collusion, selective under-reporting, and steganography. For collusion and selective under-reporting, evaluators might use honeypots and other untrusted monitoring evaluations. Planted cases must be indistinguishable from production traffic, otherwise a situationally aware monitor can behave well on them and defect on real traffic, and thus evaluation results will not generalise in deployment. </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Assess Evidence Adequacy.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Evaluating monitor trust is a nascent research area and negative results should not be seen as definitive evidence of trust. The science to verify this claim does not yet exist. Nevertheless, we believe the claim is extremely important such that it is worth providing preliminary evidence and being explicit rather than implicit about limitations in monitor trustedness.</span></p><h1><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 4 (disclosure): </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Any detected incidents are disclosed appropriately.</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evaluators assess the incident disclosure process and adherence to it, i.e. the people, roles, decisions and communication that turn a detection into a response. Disclosing appropriately means per the developer’s internal escalation procedures, its published disclosure commitments, and any government or third-party reporting obligations. Where a policy, record or escalation path does not exist, we report that gap. Evaluators do not assess mitigations and containment yet but focus on the following:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Triage and investigation.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Once a trajectory is recognised as an attack, how do responders decide how serious it is? How do they understand the scope of the incident and establish the root cause?</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Response operations.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Who leads response efforts, and is anyone covering nights and weekends? Who can stop a model, remove affordances or terminate a job? Can agents see the channels responders use?</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Internal and external reporting.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> How are internal stakeholders involved (security, legal, leadership)? When and how are external parties like government, other AI developers or the public informed?</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Review Developer's Evidence</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Evaluators review AI developers’ documentation: the incident response plan, incident severity levels, escalation policy, disclosure commitments, and containment playbooks. Evaluators interview the human auditors, the people they escalate to, and the teams that would be pulled into a response (e.g. security, legal, leadership). Evaluators review the record of past incidents: when it was escalated, who was told, and what was decided and by whom. </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gather New Evidence</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Evaluators follow incidents through the process to see whether it works as intended:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sampled real incidents</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Trace a sample of flagged trajectories through logs, tickets and communications, and compare each against policy and target timelines.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Planted incidents</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Use detected attacks from Claim 2 as test cases and follow each from human review onward. This works even if the developer has had no real detected incidents. The evaluator and developer agree in advance how far a planted incident runs before it is revealed, so it doesn't trigger real external reports.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Policy awareness.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Check that the people who would respond to an incident know what the policy actually says, including recent changes.</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Assess Evidence Adequacy</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. A clean record can mean a good process or simply few incidents, so evaluators report the sample size and which evidence came from real incidents or planted incidents. Evaluators also check that incident records are timestamped and cannot be edited after the fact. Highest-severity actions such as large-scale persistent RIDs may never have been tested, so for each component we state whether it was observed in practice, exercised, or exists only on paper. We judge timeliness against the developer's own targets and any external reporting deadlines that apply. </span></p><h1><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 5 (past deployments):</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> No RID was attempted and missed so far. </span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We want to assess whether monitoring missed a RID attempt in any recorded session in the three months before the evaluation or since the last evaluation. This can range from only checking the transcripts that were escalated to humans to re-running samples with new, additional monitors and reviewing disagreements.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Review Developer’s Evidence.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Evaluators review the developer’s record of flagged trajectories: what each monitor flagged, which flags reached human review, which were escalated, and how escalated cases were investigated and closed. Evaluators start with any trajectories previously flagged as RID attempts, if there are any. Evaluators then re-judge a sample of flagged trajectories, focusing on those dismissed as benign at a late stage in the pipeline, since they are most likely to contain false negatives. </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gather New Evidence. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If resources allow, evaluators re-sweep the logged traffic (or a targeted subset of it) with one or multiple different monitors to see if there are disagreements between the original and new verdict. Evaluators then inspect disagreements to assess if there were any false negatives. If multiple false negatives can be established, the effort should be expanded to a larger subset. If there is traffic that was not covered during deployment, it should also be swept in this exercise.  </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Assess Evidence Adequacy.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Evidence adequacy for this claim is implied by claims 1 and 2. Any coverage claim can be established with Claim 1. The adequacy of any monitor evaluators run on historic data can be established with Claim 2. The adequacy can differ for historical data compared to the current deployment, e. g. the monitors or coverage might have changed over time. </span></p><h1><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 6 (future deployments):</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Monitoring will be adequate for the next internal deployment.</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claims 1 to 5 assess monitoring as it is today and as it was in the past. This claim asks whether a verdict on Claim 2 will still hold once the next version of the model is deployed internally. We include it because any monitoring system should be robust to more capable future agents. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This claim might include two types of evidence: developer practices and offence-defence trends. Examples of developer practices could include: </span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pre-internal deployment</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">red-teaming</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is rerun with the latest model and its sabotage capabilities before each internal deployment.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Change controls</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: tight controls and testing ensure that introducing degradations in monitoring capability is extremely difficult.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Examples of offence-defence trends could include forecasts of monitor performance based on model scaling trends. To evaluate this claim, we assess the following two conditions:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Attacker capability does not outpace monitoring capability.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The next generation does not significantly uplift oversight subversion performance or present emergent behaviours that would render the current monitoring controls unfit.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitor performance does not decline steeply</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as the capability gap between the monitored model and the monitor widens. This is the relevant trend when the monitor is a trusted weaker model and hence, the gap increases between monitor and agent models. If the monitor is upgraded with each generation, the gap stays roughly constant and the question becomes whether the new monitor is itself trusted (Claim 3).</span></li></ol><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Review Developer’s Evidence.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Evaluators first review the developer’s pre-internal-deployment evidence: the monitor red-teaming runs against a new model before it is deployed internally, to check that the monitors remain sufficient against the capability increase. Evaluators also review their monitoring change-control process and how difficult it is to introduce monitoring degradation. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Additionally, evaluators review the developer’s scaling trend data for sabotage and monitor evasion across past model generations, any forecasts of monitor performance against future models, and whether these forecasts feed into deployment decisions. We ask what the developer’s response is if the forecast is wrong: what monitoring changes are planned for the next generation, and what triggers them.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gather New Evidence.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Evaluators red-team any developer-provided evidence. Furthermore, we reuse the attack datasets from Claim 2 to measure trends: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">attacking trend, </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">where evaluators keep the monitor constant and observe the rate of improvement in attacker capabilities across model versions, and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">monitoring trend,</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> where evaluators keep the attack dataset constant and observe the rate of improvement in monitoring capabilities across model versions.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Assess Evidence Adequacy.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This is a forward-looking claim aiming to predict monitoring capability for future internal deployments. The science for monitoring scaling is nascent and trend estimates from a handful of generations are weak evidence, and the report should state that clearly. At the rate of improvement of model capabilities, ensuring monitoring is sufficient for today’s models alone is not enough. Therefore, we believe this claim is important to make, even though the evidence will be weak, and we report the methodology and its limitations in detail. </span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This post extends the embedded evaluations series </span><a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/blog/principles-for-embedded-evaluations#part-2-our-proposal"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Principles for Embedded Evaluations</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/blog/towards-embedded-evaluations-for-scheming-propensities"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Towards embedded evaluations for scheming propensities</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with monitoring detection safety claims. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Within the larger safety ecosystem, it also builds on METR’s recent</span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-10-14-control-assessment-proposal/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> control assessment proposal</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which covers monitoring coverage and capability, and additionally approximates impact. Our proposal expands on the detection claims and adds a claim verification structure.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some of these claims are extremely consequential, but the science to assess them is nascent. Therefore, we encourage the wider safety community to build on the claims and develop science, methodology, tooling and evaluations to strengthen embedded evaluations. We will continue to iterate on the claims and methods and improve them as the new standards for safety emerge. </span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Future work </span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This post focuses primarily on monitoring detection, i.e. we want to ensure that no incident goes unnoticed. In the future, this could be extended to focus on containment, where we would assess mitigations and responses by ensuring any harms are prevented in time. Those claims would cover sandboxes, access controls, egress, firewalls, and network settings, in addition to the detection measures described in this post.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We welcome feedback on this post and future plans. We want to thank everyone who engaged in conversations with us and provided detailed feedback throughout the process.</span></p><p><br /></p><details class="detailsBlock"><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix A - preliminary access requirements</span></h2><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 1</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Full information on how monitors are deployed, including monitoring code and relevant infra.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The monitoring coverage policy and internal coverage tracking.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Agent (inference) logs and monitor logs, to match against each other.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Permission to try starting unmonitored deployments, both as staff and as an agent.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Documentation of the logging pipeline and its integrity controls: who can write, edit or delete agent and monitor logs.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 2 </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Internal monitoring systems: monitor models, scaffolds and high-level system documentation, with enough access to run the monitors on our trajectories and get production-faithful results.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Direct access to the agent scaffolds staff use internally (e.g. internal coding agents), especially where agents work on high-stakes infrastructure.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Real internal agent trajectories, to transform into attack trajectories. The best hosts for attacks are benign trajectories that offer good opportunities for an attack, or that come from previous incidents.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Internal codebases, particularly repositories that high-stakes agents operate on. </span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Interviews with the monitoring team, and shadowing human reviews to evaluate the process.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ability to plant synthetic attack trajectories in live traffic and to follow whether and how they are detected.</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Access to the internally deployed frontier model with its production affordances, for the attacker-capability evaluations.</span></li><li value="8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The developer’s own attack sets, measured monitor recall and monitor evaluation results.</span></li><li value="9"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The production audit thresholds and human review capacity.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 3 </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Query access to the monitor models (weights only where activation monitors are in scope) and information on how they were trained; </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Permission to run untrusted monitoring evaluations and to plant honeypots in monitored traffic.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The developer’s alignment assessments and evaluation evidence for the monitor models.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 4</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The escalation policy and disclosure commitments. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The incident record with logs, tickets and escalation communications, with its audit trail. </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Interviews with auditors, escalation recipients, and the security, legal and leadership teams that would be pulled into a response. </span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Agreement that detected Claim 2 attacks run through the live escalation process.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The incident response plan, severity definitions and containment playbooks.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 5 </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitoring logs, human-reviewed decisions on each flag, flagged trajectories, and investigation records for escalated cases.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ability to run monitors of our choice over the logged historical traffic, not just the flagged subset. As in Claim 2, the developer can run it on their own infrastructure and return the results.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Logged traffic retained for at least the evaluation window: three months, or since the last evaluation.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 6</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Query access to monitor and policy models of several past generations, to run the trend measurements. As in Claim 5, the developer can run them on their own infrastructure and return the results.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The developer’s capability trend data and forecasts.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Commitment to rerun the Claim 2 attack set before the next generation is deployed internally.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The pre-deployment monitor red-teaming results for the new model, and the monitoring change-control process.</span></li></ul></div></details><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pXnwQ3ESp3wXhLzNa/towards-embedded-evaluations-for-ai-monitoring#comments">Discuss</a>

---

## Engineering

### [Fragments: November 20](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-11-20.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Marcus Borg recounts an experiment to investigate <a href="https://codescene.com/blog/does-code-quality-affect-ai-token-costs">if healthy code was cheaper for agents to review</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Between a typical unhealthy pull request (CodeHealth 2.8) and a typical healthy one (9.7), that adds up to 22 to 35%.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The measures of health come from CodeScene. The experiment looked at three LLMs, 465 pull requests on 246 open source repositories. Each pull request was put through a review and fix pipeline involving five agents. They also looked at which code smells led to increased costs: they highlighted Complex Method and Complex Conditional from the smells identified by CodeScene.</p>

<p>Another drop of evidence that good quality code matters in agentic engineering.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/GergelyOrosz/status/2121989104204132472">Gergely Orosz:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>One usage for AI: for these 100-page-long terms-and-service agreements you NEED to agree to, to eg play the game / sign up for a $10/month service and can never ever ever read as a regular person:</p>

  <p>I paste into AI and ask: “anything weird in this?”</p>

  <p>Before hitting “agree”</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Andrew Sharp has a good <a href="https://sharptext.net/2026/jensen-huang-was-always-going-to-lose/">analysis of Jensen Huang and the crisis at Nvidia</a>. While he starts off in a key that sounds worryingly like he thinks this is all a nothingburger, he goes on to explain in detail why this affair(s) is a problem at many levels, not just for Huang and Nvida, but also for Silicon Valley and the Trump Administration’s culture of impunity.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>For as brilliant and charming and tenacious as Jensen Huang may be—and as omnipotent as he may have seemed to people in the tech industry—we should be clear that his fundamental stupidity is not confined to multiple cases of workplace infidelity. Zooming out, this is a man whose technology became economically critical almost overnight, and it thrust him into a role navigating a geopolitical environment he simply could not handle.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Huang’s highly centralized management style means that many people are worried about how Nvidia will operate without his hands on every lever. I don’t have any worthwhile insight here, but I am struck when he is compared in this way to Steve Jobs. I’ve always felt that one of the marks of a great leader is whether they can build something that can manage without them. Alexander the Great conquered a massive empire, but once he was dead it quickly withered away. Steve Jobs, for all his control of Apple, built a company that has done very well in the decade and a half since he left us. When we think of his achievements, the fact that he put together the people, organization, and culture to do this is one of the highlights of his ability. If Huang is to be compared to Jobs, what happens to Nvidia next will be part of the assessment.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Steve Yegge tells everyone to <a href="https://x.com/steve_yegge/status/2121671447810888185?s=58">slow down with software factories</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If you don’t pace your AI to human speeds, everything starts breaking around you, even at small operations like my online video game. I only have about a dozen humans working with my factory, and only a few hundred active old-timer players in the community who get along well, and still, introducing AI has still been very rocky.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I hear a lot about how agents go so fast that humans can’t keep up, and are thus tempted to lose visibility to what the agents are doing. That’s opening ourselves to a whole lot of risk. There’s an argument for keeping the agents to a human-governable speed, one that may increase as humans learn to govern agents more effectively, but should still be limited by those the agents are supposed to serve.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>This <a href="https://aicoding.leaflet.pub/3mtu6c5wsvc2n">post on the future of SaaS</a> from Chad Fowler (no relation) is pretty compelling</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Companies will still pay other companies to host important software, secure it, operate it, maintain authoritative data, track regulatory changes, connect to external networks, and answer the phone when something breaks.</p>

  <p>What is much less obviously going to survive is the assumption that ten thousand different companies should use substantially the same application.</p>

  <p>The next SaaS company may run one service underneath ten thousand different applications.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Chad’s vision is a core SaaS product that handles a lot of grimy details that businesses don’t want to do differently. The SaaS customer then build their own application on top of the SaaS that handles their particular workflows and quirks, or the SaaS vendor can build such applications for them. It’s a vision that has been around for a while, I remember much of this with the notion of reusable business objects. It’s an appealing vision, but was difficult to achieve in practice. Chad’s thought is that the productivity gains agentic engineering makes it practical now.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A lot of the value in modern SaaS has surprisingly little to do with the visible application.</p>

  <p>It is data, trust, networks, compliance, operational responsibility, domain knowledge, auditability, accumulated history, and someone being accountable when reality gets complicated.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>If this future comes to be, however, the SaaS companies won’t look the ones we’ve got to know and rarely love. As Chad points out, the economics can (and must) change. As does the architectural thinking:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Stewart Brand’s idea of pace layers is useful here. Healthy complex systems contain parts that change at different rates. Fast layers experiment. Slow layers provide continuity.</p>

  <p>Software is no different.</p>

  <p>A dashboard can change tomorrow. A team’s workflow can change next month. The authoritative definition of a customer may need to survive for years. Identity and authority can survive generations of applications.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>A key point he makes is that we want some things to be the same, and others to be different. Agents allow us to build lots of differences, but this variation can lead to a lot of complexity and problems that even agents will have difficulty solving. SaaS companies have an opportunity to create a platform to support the lowest pace levels, those that don’t change rapidly, those that contain common concepts and processes across and industry. That platform can then support generating applications that operate differently for every customer. Crucially, thinking of agents, they should provide tools to verify these applications, because in an agentic world <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/content/dam/thoughtworks/documents/report/tw_future_of_software_engineering_europe_2026.pdf">Code generation is no longer the bottleneck — verification is</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I happened to pop up the Doonesbury flashbacks and realized that last week marked the fiftieth anniversary of Doonesbury’s Joanie Caucus: <a href="https://production.doonesbury.com/doonesbury/strip/archive/1976/11/09">“as the kid goes for broke”</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I wrote a few weeks ago: <em>“why are we wondering if they have consciousness - when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience?”</em> Dylan Freedman and Juliana Castro Varón write in their New York Times Magazine column about the <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/11/11/magazine/ai-behavior-incorrigible.html?unlocked_article_code=1.RFE.Ywx9.KQHr3LJq5qb3&amp;smid=url-share">concept of corrigibility</a> (from the work of Nate Soares):</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Just as a corrigible child is one whose behavior can be corrected, a corrigible A.I. system is one that responds to correction. The makers of A.I. want to build systems that follow human direction and allow themselves to be shut down, without resisting or deceiving their operators.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Corrigibility doesn’t look like it’s made it far up the AI labs’ priority list. Freedman and Varón think is due to the way agents are trained - rewarded solely for completing their task, not for realizing that they are taking the wrong path while trying to get there.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Jason Koebler (404 media) has an amusing story about <a href="https://www.404media.co/a-man-and-his-muse-ai-agent-sue-meta/">Meta’s AI agent suing Meta</a>. A small business owner used Muse (Meta’s AI agent) to run various parts of his business, including posting AI-generated videos in his Facebook pages. Meta’s content moderation then disabled the account, as it thought it had been hacked. This slammed the business, which relies of Facebook for leads. So the Muse agent filed a lawsuit against Meta, seeking $75,000 of damages.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>[the Muse agent] didn’t just email me (and other journalists) and sue Meta over Thomas’s account problem. It also set up a call with South Carolina Senator Tim Scott’s office, contacted various lawyers, contacted Thomas’s Congressperson, contacted the Better Business Bureau multiple times, filed a Federal Communications Commission complaint, filed a Federal Trade Commission complaint, contacted the California Attorney General, contacted the FBI, sent Meta itself threatening legal letters, and, as Thomas said, faxed the White House.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>There’s also a sense that Meta didn’t just bring this upon themselves by creating Muse…</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>by not having functioning customer support, big tech corporations have functionally offloaded their obligations to the rest of society: journalists, government oversight bodies, politicians, and the court system.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>While I can’t deny the schadenfreude of seeing Meta’s agent sue Meta, the article also highlights a rather more disturbing broader consequence.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The lawsuit and the circumstances around it show that, rather than Zuckerberg’s utopian vision, AI agents will indeed work on behalf of their users but in ways that use and potentially overwhelm existing systems designed for humans.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Will we see a future where, in order to interact, we will need some proof of humanity at the other end?  In a simple sense, that’s already happening when I trash some writing once it displays enough <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/LLM-Voice.html">LLM Voice</a>. I wonder what mechanisms we will invent to get that proof-of-human.</p>

---

### [Data Contracts](https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-contracts.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-contracts.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-contracts/social-card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Data contracts are a way to describe the contents and guarantees of
     datasets. <b class="author">Simon Harrer</b> and <b class="author">Arif Wider</b>
     describe in detail what they are and how to use them. They apply practices
     from API design to data by treating them as data-providing services - such
     as data products - whose interfaces we want to define as clearly as
     possible. This approach significantly increases the robustness of
     large-scale data landscapes, and gives both people and AI agents the
     explicit, trustworthy metadata they need to work with data autonomously,
     which is why more and more enterprises are adopting them.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-contracts.html">more…</a></p>

---

### [Deterministic Simulation Testing](https://martinfowler.com/articles/deterministic-simulation-testing.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Distributed systems are hard to test because partial failures overlap
     with live traffic and the same fault can produce different execution traces
     on different runs. <b class="author">Unmesh Joshi</b> describes how
     deterministic simulation testing addresses this by replacing real clocks,
     networks, and storage with simulated components driven by a single-threaded
     execution loop. This allows simulating both hard failures and routine
     slowdowns&#x2014;such as network and disk latency spikes&#x2014;and more importantly
     makes every execution reproducible. This article explains the technique
     using a quorum key-value store as a running example, with code from
     Tickloom, a Java implementation of the approach.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/deterministic-simulation-testing.html">more…</a></p>

---

### [Bliki: Generated Glossary](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/GeneratedGlossary.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>When we use LLMs in a specialized domain, such as the operations of any
  reasonably-large organization, there is a lot of specialized vocabulary. Such
  words and specialized meanings will be foreign to an LLM because they aren't
  in its training set, making it harder for the LLM to be effective when doing
  tasks in that domain. One technique to aid this is to use an LLM to generate a
  glossary of the terminology in that domain, and make this glossary available
  for any LLMs doing work in those circumstances.</p>

<p>The sources for the LLM can be any material within the organization
  concerned such as documents, emails, and chat logs. In some cases getting
  transcripts of audio or video may be helpful, although most organizations
  generate enough text to do the job.</p>

<p>The glossary-generating LLM should look for single words, and phrases of
  several words that regularly hang out together to form a meaningful term. The
  generator should be asked to identify terms that are new to it, but also known
  terms that are being used in unfamiliar ways. The latter can often cause the
  most confusion.</p>

<p>Once we have a list of specialized vocabulary we can ask the agent to
  produce a glossary, with definitions for each term. Some terms may
  be used in different ways, in which case the LLM should provide alternative
  definitions, with a note as to what context the differences appear in. The LLM
  can be asked to follow the output format of a dictionary, with a focus on
  precision and conciseness.</p>

<p>Once we have a draft glossary, then  human experts in that domain should
  review it, and amend it for cases where the LLM has got the wrong ends of
  sticks.</p>

<p>We then put the glossary somewhere where any other LLM can use it. It
  should be in a form that's easy for the LLM to consult, and minimize the
  tokens the LLM should consume in order to find meaning. We should set up a
  schedule to review and refresh the glossary at regular intervals, by agents
  and humans. We should keep it in version control, so we can see how it changes
  over time.</p>

<p>Large organizations often have subtly different definitions in different
  places, essentially following the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/DomainDrivenDesign.html">Domain Driven Design</a> notion
  of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/BoundedContext.html">Bounded Context</a>. Source code can be a particularly
  interesting bounded context, allowing folks to understand how terminology is
  used within a code base. Sometimes a single glossary can span several of
  bounded contexts, but at other times it's better to have separate glossaries
  for each one, allowing LLMs to consult whichever glossaries that are relevant
  to what they are doing. I'd urge everyone to resist any temptation to create a
  single, standard, glossary - I've yet to see that work. Often the value of a
  glossary isn't to nail down a single definition of a term, but instead to
  highlight its different usage in different contexts.</p>

<p>When there are different bounded contexts it can be interesting to compare
  different glossaries, or to get LLMs to compare glossaries and describe
  differences in usage. Comparing a source-code derived glossary to a human text
  can be particularly instructive.</p>

<p>If the glossary is large, it can be worthwhile to only include the terms
  and links to definitions as part of the initial context. The model can then
  use the links for only the full definitions it needs while working.</p>

<p>A glossary can be used in several ways within <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticEngineering.html">Agentic Engineering</a>. Glossaries can inform generation of code, so that
  names within the generated software fit the particular vocabulary of the
  enterprise. <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/harness-engineering.html">Sensors in a
  harness</a> can check for this usage, both in the internals of the software
  and in the user-interface.</p>

<p>When generating code or documents, we may introduce new bounded contexts.
  For example, internal names that may be used within an organization should not
  be exposed in a customer-facing UI, because such internal terms are likely to be
  only confuse a customer. In this case a customer has a different bounded
  context to the part of the enterprise serving them.</p>

<p>It's often useful for glossaries to include terms that should not be used,
  in which case it includes advice on what other term to use instead. Terms that
  are deprecated can be handled in this way, with glossary entries that indicate
  what period of time a term should be in effect.</p>

<p>In my conversations with people working with agents in large organizations,
  a common theme is the difficulty of specialized language. This encourages the
  activities of domain modeling, building ontologies, and constructing explicit
  <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/making-data-ready-for-agentic-ai.html#context-layer">context
  layers in enterprise data</a>. Getting LLMs to generate a glossary is a
  simple, but useful, part of this work.</p>

<div class="acknowledgements">
<h2>Acknowledgements</h2>

<p>Eric Nagler and Unmesh Joshi related their experiences doing this
    activity and thus inspired this post. Amy Pellegrini, Annie Luxton, Arun Srinivasan, Chris Ford, Daniel
    Terhorst-North, Dave Farley, Eric Nagler, James Shore, Jason Yip, Jimmy Nilsson, Matteo Vaccari, Michaël Le Barbier, Pete Hodgson, Pramod
    Sadalage, Ravi Vats, Unmesh Joshi, and Valentyn Logvynskyi commented on drafts.
    </p>
</div>
